I don’t know… I mean, I guess I haven’t really been paying attention to that. I used to like little things, like walking around near the river after work or looking at old building designs online — which sounds boring, I know, but I actually liked seeing how people solved things.

Lately it’s more like… I don’t even get that far. I’ll open a tab or put on a show and then realize I’m just staring at it. But I don’t know if that means I’m not enjoying it, or if I’m just distracted. Everything sort of blurs together.

Everything sort of blurs together.\n\n[Last Question]\nAre there things that you still find pleasure in doing?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 34 / civil_engineer / bachelors\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared.   },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not really sure. That sounds like it should be an easy question, but I keep trying to think of something and then I get stuck on whether I actually enjoy it or I just do it because it’s there.

Like, I made coffee this morning and I used to sort of like the routine of that — the grinder, the smell, all of it — but then I was just thinking about how the kitchen still has this one cabinet door that never closes right, and my ex always said we’d fix it, and then suddenly I’m not even drinking the coffee, I’m just standing there. So… I don’t know. Maybe little moments? But nothing that really feels like pleasure in the way it used to.

I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean… maybe? It’s hard to tell whether I’ve lost interest or whether everything just feels like too much effort to start.

I don’t really know. I keep thinking there should be something, like… even something small, but when I try to name it, my mind kind of wanders off.

Sometimes I think maybe I’ll look forward to finishing a work thing, but then I start thinking about this bridge project from years ago where we kept finding problems in the drainage plans, and everyone acted like it was a big mystery when it was obviously the grading, and— sorry, that’s not really what you asked.

I guess I don’t notice myself looking forward to much. Or if I do, it doesn’t stay in my head long enough for me to recognize it as that.

Yeah… I mean, not exactly like… I don’t know. I haven’t been thinking, “I’m going to do this specific thing,” if that’s what you mean.

But I have been thinking it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up. Which sounds so dramatic when I say it out loud. I kept trying to tell myself it was just a passing thought, like background noise, but then it got… louder, I guess. Intense enough that I scared myself.

I ended up calling the employee assistance program, actually. I almost didn’t, because their website is this awful maze of links and forms, and I kept thinking about how someone approved that layout somehow— sorry. The point is, yes. I have had thoughts like that, and it got bad enough that I reached out.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
More like speeding up, I think. Not moving slowly.

I’ve been… pushing myself into everything. Like extra proposals at work, even ones that weren’t really mine to touch, and calling colleagues because I’d suddenly have some idea that felt important enough that I had to say it right then. Which is embarrassing, because people are busy, and also some of them don’t answer their phones anymore, they just let it go to voicemail like it’s 2009 or something.

And I volunteered for tasks no one asked me to take on. Just kept adding things. Then at home, when there wasn’t anything useful left to do, I’d just pace around the apartment, kind of looking for the next thing. Like my body was still trying to solve a problem even after the problem was gone.

Yeah… yes. A lot.

I keep turning things over in my head and somehow it all becomes evidence that I’m just… bad at everything. Like I’ll think, I’m a terrible engineer, because of one calculation I missed or almost missed, or an email that sounded sharper than I meant it to. Pretty often. I mean… daily, probably. It’s like my brain keeps a running list of reasons I’m not doing anything right.

I’ll tell myself I’m a terrible engineer because of one missed calculation or some email that came out too tense, and then I’ll start going back through everything like I’m checking a structure for cracks, except the structure is just me, I guess. And every little thing becomes proof.

Like debris everywhere, and I’m supposed to just keep going to work and answer emails about drainage plans and concrete specs like any of it matters.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this heavy, hopeless feeling?\n\nPatient: About a month, I think. It sort of settled in around when the financial stuff from the divorce got really messy — the bills and accounts and all those stupid forms. I remember staring at a spreadsheet one night, and not even for work, just my own life, and thinking, “How is this what everything turned into?” Since then it’s just been... there. Like a weight.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like before this heavy feeling started?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I guess I haven’t really been paying attention to that. I used to like little things, like walking around near the river after work or looking at old building designs online — which sounds boring, I know, but I actually liked seeing how people solved things.\n\nLately it’s more like… I don’t even get that far. I’ll open a tab or put on a show and then realize I’m just staring at it. But I don’t know if that means I’m not enjoying it, or if I’m just distracted. Everything sort of blurs together.\n\nDoctor: Are there things that you still find pleasure in doing?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. That sounds like it should be an easy question, but I keep trying to think of something and then I get stuck on whether I actually enjoy it or I just do it because it’s there.\n\nLike, I made coffee this morning and I used to sort of like the routine of that — the grinder, the smell, all of it — but then I was just thinking about how the kitchen still has this one cabinet door that never closes right, and my ex always said we’d fix it, and then suddenly I’m not even drinking the coffee, I’m just standing there. So… I don’t know. Maybe little moments? It’s hard to tell whether I’ve lost interest or whether everything just feels like too much effort to start.\n\nLike with the river walks, I used to do them without thinking, and now I’ll think, “I should go,” and then I remember I need to respond to an email or I see laundry on the chair, and suddenly it’s an hour later and I haven’t moved. I’ve always had piles of stuff when a project gets intense — at work we used to joke my desk looked like a landslide model, which, actually, was kind of accurate.\n\nSo… I don’t know. Or if I do, it doesn’t stay in my head long enough for me to recognize it as that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about harming yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not exactly like… I don’t know. Yeah… restless, I guess. Not just like tapping my foot, though I do that too sometimes. It’s more like I can’t leave things alone.

I’ll open a tab or put on a show and then realize I’m just staring at it. But I don’t know if that means I’m not enjoying it, or if I’m just distracted. Everything sort of blurs together.\n\nDoctor: Are there things that you still find pleasure in doing?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. That sounds like it should be an easy question, but I keep trying to think of something and then I get stuck on whether I actually enjoy it or I just do it because it’s there.\n\nLike, I made coffee this morning and I used to sort of like the routine of that — the grinder, the smell, all of it — but then I was just thinking about how the kitchen still has this one cabinet door that never closes right, and my ex always said we’d fix it, and then suddenly I’m not even drinking the coffee, I’m just standing there. So… I don’t know. Maybe little moments? But nothing that really feels like pleasure in the way it used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean… maybe? It’s hard to tell whether I’ve lost interest or whether everything just feels like too much effort to start.\n\nLike with the river walks, I used to do them without thinking, and now I’ll think, “I should go,” and then I remember I need to respond to an email or I see laundry on the chair, and suddenly it’s an hour later and I haven’t moved. But that could just be me being disorganized. I’ve always had piles of stuff when a project gets intense — at work we used to joke my desk looked like a landslide model, which, actually, was kind of accurate.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I haven’t really been reaching for those things, I guess. Whether that’s lost interest or just… getting stuck, I’m not totally sure.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still look forward to doing?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I keep thinking there should be something, like… even something small, but when I try to name it, my mind kind of wanders off.\n\nSometimes I think maybe I’ll look forward to finishing a work thing, but then I start thinking about this bridge project from years ago where we kept finding problems in the drainage plans, and everyone acted like it was a big mystery when it was obviously the grading, and— sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I don’t notice myself looking forward to much. Or if I do, it doesn’t stay in my head long enough for me to recognize it as that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about harming yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not exactly like… I don’t know. I haven’t been thinking, “I’m going to do this specific thing,” if that’s what you mean.\n\nBut I have been thinking it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up. Which sounds so dramatic when I say it out loud. I kept trying to tell myself it was just a passing thought, like background noise, but then it got… louder, I guess. Intense enough that I scared myself.\n\nI ended up calling the employee assistance program, actually. I almost didn’t, because their website is this awful maze of links and forms, and I kept thinking about how someone approved that layout somehow— sorry. The point is, yes. I have had thoughts like that, and it got bad enough that I reached out.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t been tracking it or anything. My scale is in the bathroom closet behind this stack of cleaning supplies I keep meaning to reorganize, so I don’t really use it.\n\nMy clothes… maybe they feel the same? I don’t know. I haven’t noticed a clear change, at least not one I could put a number on.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yeah. Sleep has been… pretty bad, honestly.\n\nSome nights I’m just lying there staring at the ceiling, going over bills or something I might’ve messed up at work, and then it turns into this whole mental spreadsheet I can’t shut off. Or I’ll finally sleep, but then I wake up before dawn and feel like I didn’t actually sleep at all.\n\nAnd then I start noticing stupid things, like the little crack in the ceiling paint, and I’m thinking about whether that’s moisture or just settling, because in buildings it’s never “just a crack,” and— sorry. Yes. I’ve been having trouble sleeping.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly than usual or feeling like you're speeding up in your thoughts or actions?\n\nPatient: More like speeding up, I think. Not moving slowly.\n\nI’ve been… pushing myself into everything. Like extra proposals at work, even ones that weren’t really mine to touch, and calling colleagues because I’d suddenly have some idea that felt important enough that I had to say it right then. Which is embarrassing, because people are busy, and also some of them don’t answer their phones anymore, they just let it go to voicemail like it’s 2009 or something.\n\nAnd I volunteered for tasks no one asked me to take on. Just kept adding things. Then at home, when there wasn’t anything useful left to do, I’d just pace around the apartment, kind of looking for the next thing. Like my body was still trying to solve a problem even after the problem was gone.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for things that have happened?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. A lot.\n\nI keep turning things over in my head and somehow it all becomes evidence that I’m just… bad at everything. Like I’ll think, I’m a terrible engineer, because of one calculation I missed or almost missed, or an email that sounded sharper than I meant it to. And then I start replaying every tense message, every decision, like I’m building a case against myself.\n\nAnd it’s not just work. I think I’m a failed wife, too. Like I should have been easier to live with, or more stable, or— I don’t know. Even little things become this proof that I ruin things. I feel like a burden. Then I’ll remember some random detail, like the wording in a project comment from months ago, and it just loops.\n\nDoctor: How often do you find yourself thinking you're a failure or that you're worthless?\n\nPatient: Pretty often. And every little thing becomes proof.\n\nAnd with my marriage, I keep thinking I was a failed wife, that I was too much or not enough or just… a burden. It’s not like one thought and then it passes. Yes. That’s actually a good way to put it — like I’m constantly “on,” even when there isn’t a real reason to be.

At work I started pushing myself into extra proposals, calling colleagues with new ideas, volunteering for tasks nobody asked me to take on. I don’t think so… not voices, like someone talking to me. I mean, I get thoughts that feel loud, if that makes sense, but they still feel like mine.

Sometimes when I’m stressed I’ll replay conversations in my head, especially work stuff — like a meeting where I should’ve said something differently, or the way someone paused before answering me, and then I start analyzing it like it’s a structural failure report. But that’s not the same as hearing something, I don’t think.

So… no, I haven’t heard voices that aren’t mine. At least not that I’ve noticed.

Or I’ll finally sleep, but then I wake up before dawn and feel like I didn’t actually sleep at all.\n\nAnd then I start noticing stupid things, like the little crack in the ceiling paint, and I’m thinking about whether that’s moisture or just settling, because in buildings it’s never “just a crack,” and— sorry. Yes. Not moving slowly.\n\nI’ve been… pushing myself into everything. Like extra proposals at work, even ones that weren’t really mine to touch, and calling colleagues because I’d suddenly have some idea that felt important enough that I had to say it right then. Just kept adding things. Then at home, when there wasn’t anything useful left to do, I’d just pace around the apartment, kind of looking for the next thing. Like my body was still trying to solve a problem even after the problem was gone.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for things that have happened?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. And then when there wasn’t anything useful left to do, I’d just pace around my apartment like I was waiting for some problem to appear so I could fix it.\n\nWhich is ridiculous because my apartment isn’t even that big, so it’s like… kitchen, window, hallway, back again. Like I’m inspecting a site with no actual site. But yes, restless. Definitely.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you're constantly on the go or can't sit still?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually a good way to put it — like I’m constantly “on,” even when there isn’t a real reason to be.\n\nAt work I started pushing myself into extra proposals, calling colleagues with new ideas, volunteering for tasks nobody asked me to take on. Which, I mean, sometimes that looks productive from the outside, so people don’t immediately question it. Engineers are always supposed to be busy, right? There’s always some deadline or drawing set or meeting that could’ve been an email.\n\nBut even when I got home, if there wasn’t anything useful left to do, I’d pace around my apartment. Just back and forth, like my body didn’t get the memo that the workday was over. It’s embarrassing because I’d catch myself doing it and think, okay, sit down like a normal person, and then two minutes later I’d be up again.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any thoughts or heard any voices that aren't your own?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not voices, like someone talking to me. I mean, I get thoughts that feel loud, if that makes sense, but they still feel like mine.\n\nSometimes when I’m stressed I’ll replay conversations in my head, especially work stuff — like a meeting where I should’ve said something differently, or the way someone paused before answering me, and then I start analyzing it like it’s a structural failure report. But that’s not the same as hearing something, I don’t think.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t heard voices that aren’t mine. At least not that I’ve noticed.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like your heavy, hopeless feeling is interfering with your ability to function in daily life?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… it has. I mean, I wanted to say I was still functioning because I was still showing up, still answering emails, still putting on work clothes and pretending that counted. But it really wasn’t okay.

At work I started making calls I probably shouldn’t have been making — project decisions, judgment things — and then I’d fall apart in meetings. Not in some dramatic movie way, just… I couldn’t hold it together or think clearly. My supervisor actually had to take me off active project decisions, which was humiliating. Civil engineering is all about being reliable, you know, like if you miss one load calculation— sorry, that’s not the point.

At home it got worse too. I stopped keeping up with basic stuff, like routines, regular life things. Everything felt too heavy to do. And eventually I ended up being sent to the emergency department because I didn’t feel safe with my thoughts. So… yes. It’s interfering a lot.

Like debris everywhere, and I’m supposed to just keep going to work and answer emails about drainage plans and concrete specs like any of it matters.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this heavy, hopeless feeling?\n\nPatient: About a month, I think. It sort of settled in around when the financial stuff from the divorce got really messy — the bills and accounts and all those stupid forms. I remember staring at a spreadsheet one night, and not even for work, just my own life, and thinking, “How is this what everything turned into?” Since then it’s just been... there. Like a weight.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like before this heavy feeling started?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I guess I haven’t really been paying attention to that. I used to like little things, like walking around near the river after work or looking at old building designs online — which sounds boring, I know, but I actually liked seeing how people solved things.\n\nLately it’s more like… I don’t even get that far. I’ll open a tab or put on a show and then realize I’m just staring at it. But I don’t know if that means I’m not enjoying it, or if I’m just distracted. Everything sort of blurs together.\n\nDoctor: Are there things that you still find pleasure in doing?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. That sounds like it should be an easy question, but I keep trying to think of something and then I get stuck on whether I actually enjoy it or I just do it because it’s there.\n\nLike, I made coffee this morning and I used to sort of like the routine of that — the grinder, the smell, all of it — but then I was just thinking about how the kitchen still has this one cabinet door that never closes right, and my ex always said we’d fix it, and then suddenly I’m not even drinking the coffee, I’m just standing there. So… I don’t know. Maybe little moments? But nothing that really feels like pleasure in the way it used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean… maybe? It’s hard to tell whether I’ve lost interest or whether everything just feels like too much effort to start.\n\nLike with the river walks, I used to do them without thinking, and now I’ll think, “I should go,” and then I remember I need to respond to an email or I see laundry on the chair, and suddenly it’s an hour later and I haven’t moved. But that could just be me being disorganized. I’ve always had piles of stuff when a project gets intense — at work we used to joke my desk looked like a landslide model, which, actually, was kind of accurate.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I haven’t really been reaching for those things, I guess. Whether that’s lost interest or just… getting stuck, I’m not totally sure.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still look forward to doing?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I keep thinking there should be something, like… even something small, but when I try to name it, my mind kind of wanders off.\n\nSometimes I think maybe I’ll look forward to finishing a work thing, but then I start thinking about this bridge project from years ago where we kept finding problems in the drainage plans, and everyone acted like it was a big mystery when it was obviously the grading, and— sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I don’t notice myself looking forward to much. Or if I do, it doesn’t stay in my head long enough for me to recognize it as that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about harming yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not exactly like… I don’t know. I haven’t been thinking, “I’m going to do this specific thing,” if that’s what you mean.\n\nBut I have been thinking it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up. Which sounds so dramatic when I say it out loud. I kept trying to tell myself it was just a passing thought, like background noise, but then it got… louder, I guess. Intense enough that I scared myself.\n\nI ended up calling the employee assistance program, actually. I almost didn’t, because their website is this awful maze of links and forms, and I kept thinking about how someone approved that layout somehow— sorry. The point is, yes. I have had thoughts like that, and it got bad enough that I reached out.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t been tracking it or anything. My scale is in the bathroom closet behind this stack of cleaning supplies I keep meaning to reorganize, so I don’t really use it.\n\nMy clothes… maybe they feel the same? I don’t know. I haven’t noticed a clear change, at least not one I could put a number on.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yeah. Sleep has been… pretty bad, honestly.\n\nSome nights I’m just lying there staring at the ceiling, going over bills or something I might’ve messed up at work, and then it turns into this whole mental spreadsheet I can’t shut off. Or I’ll finally sleep, but then I wake up before dawn and feel like I didn’t actually sleep at all.\n\nAnd then I start noticing stupid things, like the little crack in the ceiling paint, and I’m thinking about whether that’s moisture or just settling, because in buildings it’s never “just a crack,” and— sorry. Yes. I’ve been having trouble sleeping.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly than usual or feeling like you're speeding up in your thoughts or actions?\n\nPatient: More like speeding up, I think. Not moving slowly.\n\nI’ve been… pushing myself into everything. Like extra proposals at work, even ones that weren’t really mine to touch, and calling colleagues because I’d suddenly have some idea that felt important enough that I had to say it right then. Which is embarrassing, because people are busy, and also some of them don’t answer their phones anymore, they just let it go to voicemail like it’s 2009 or something.\n\nAnd I volunteered for tasks no one asked me to take on. Just kept adding things. Then at home, when there wasn’t anything useful left to do, I’d just pace around the apartment, kind of looking for the next thing. Like my body was still trying to solve a problem even after the problem was gone.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for things that have happened?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. A lot.\n\nI keep turning things over in my head and somehow it all becomes evidence that I’m just… bad at everything. Like I’ll think, I’m a terrible engineer, because of one calculation I missed or almost missed, or an email that sounded sharper than I meant it to. And then I start replaying every tense message, every decision, like I’m building a case against myself.\n\nAnd it’s not just work. I think I’m a failed wife, too. Like I should have been easier to live with, or more stable, or— I don’t know. Even little things become this proof that I ruin things. I feel like a burden. Then I’ll remember some random detail, like the wording in a project comment from months ago, and it just loops.\n\nDoctor: How often do you find yourself thinking you're a failure or that you're worthless?\n\nPatient: Pretty often. I mean… daily, probably. It’s like my brain keeps a running list of reasons I’m not doing anything right.\n\nI’ll tell myself I’m a terrible engineer because of one missed calculation or some email that came out too tense, and then I’ll start going back through everything like I’m checking a structure for cracks, except the structure is just me, I guess. And every little thing becomes proof.\n\nAnd with my marriage, I keep thinking I was a failed wife, that I was too much or not enough or just… a burden. It’s not like one thought and then it passes. It keeps coming back. Like I ruin things.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your heavy, hopeless feeling is getting worse over time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it has. Or maybe I only notice it now because it’s gotten so heavy.\n\nThat’s kind of why I came in. I can’t shake this hopeless feeling, like everything I tried to rebuild after the divorce just… collapsed. Like when a support fails and the whole thing comes down, except it’s my life, and I’m just sitting under all of it trying to pretend I can still function. Like I should have been easier to live with, or more stable, or— I don’t know. Even little things become this proof that I ruin things. I feel like a burden. Then I’ll remember some random detail, like the wording in a project comment from months ago, and it just loops.\n\nDoctor: How often do you find yourself thinking you're a failure or that you're worthless?\n\nPatient: Pretty often. I mean… daily, probably. It’s like my brain keeps a running list of reasons I’m not doing anything right.\n\nI’ll tell myself I’m a terrible engineer because of one missed calculation or some email that came out too tense, and then I’ll start going back through everything like I’m checking a structure for cracks, except the structure is just me, I guess. And every little thing becomes proof.\n\nAnd with my marriage, I keep thinking I was a failed wife, that I was too much or not enough or just… a burden. It’s not like one thought and then it passes. It keeps coming back. Like I ruin things.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your heavy, hopeless feeling is getting worse over time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it has. Or maybe I only notice it now because it’s gotten so heavy.\n\nThat’s kind of why I came in. I can’t shake this hopeless feeling, like everything I tried to rebuild after the divorce just… collapsed. Like when a support fails and the whole thing comes down, except it’s my life, and I’m just sitting under all of it trying to pretend I can still function. And I know that sounds dramatic, but that’s how it feels.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling restless or fidgety lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… restless, I guess. Not just like tapping my foot, though I do that too sometimes. It’s more like I can’t leave things alone.\n\nI started throwing myself into extra proposals at work, calling people with ideas they didn’t even ask for, volunteering for tasks that weren’t mine. And then when there wasn’t anything useful left to do, I’d just pace around my apartment like I was waiting for some problem to appear so I could fix it.\n\nWhich is ridiculous because my apartment isn’t even that big, so it’s like… kitchen, window, hallway, back again. Like I’m inspecting a site with no actual site. But yes, restless. Definitely.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you're constantly on the go or can't sit still?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, I get thoughts that feel loud, if that makes sense, but they still feel like mine.\n\nSometimes when I’m stressed I’ll replay conversations in my head, especially work stuff — like a meeting where I should’ve said something differently, or the way someone paused before answering me, and then I start analyzing it like it’s a structural failure report. And then when there wasn’t anything useful left to do, I’d just pace around my apartment like I was waiting for some problem to appear so I could fix it.\n\nWhich is ridiculous because my apartment isn’t even that big, so it’s like… kitchen, window, hallway, back again. Like I’m inspecting a site with no actual site. But yes, restless. Definitely.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you're constantly on the go or can't sit still?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually a good way to put it — like I’m constantly “on,” even when there isn’t a real reason to be.\n\nAt work I started pushing myself into extra proposals, calling colleagues with new ideas, volunteering for tasks nobody asked me to take on. There’s always some deadline or drawing set or meeting that could’ve been an email.\n\nBut even when I got home, if there wasn’t anything useful left to do, I’d pace around my apartment. Just back and forth, like my body didn’t get the memo that the workday was over. It’s embarrassing because I’d catch myself doing it and think, okay, sit down like a normal person, and then two minutes later I’d be up again.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any thoughts or heard any voices that aren't your own?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not voices, like someone talking to me. I mean, I get thoughts that feel loud, if that makes sense, but they still feel like mine.\n\nSometimes when I’m stressed I’ll replay conversations in my head, especially work stuff — like a meeting where I should’ve said something differently, or the way someone paused before answering me, and then I start analyzing it like it’s a structural failure report. But that’s not the same as hearing something, I don’t think.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t heard voices that aren’t mine. At least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your heavy, hopeless feeling is interfering with your ability to function in daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. I mean, I wanted to say I was still functioning because I was still showing up, still answering emails, still putting on work clothes and pretending that counted. But it really wasn’t okay.\n\nAt work I started making calls I probably shouldn’t have been making — project decisions, judgment things — and then I’d fall apart in meetings. At home I stopped keeping up with basic routines and ended up being sent to the emergency department because I didn’t feel safe with my thoughts.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. I mean… yes, pretty significantly.

Work was where it showed up first, I think. I was still going in, but I wasn’t really functioning the way I’m supposed to. I started making questionable calls on project decisions, and in meetings I’d just kind of fall apart — not being able to think straight or keep myself together. My supervisor ended up taking me off active project decisions, which… in my field, that’s a big deal. It felt awful.

And at home, it wasn’t like I was recovering there either. I stopped keeping up with basic routines, like normal daily stuff. Everything just slipped. I remember thinking, if my apartment were a project site, it would’ve been shut down immediately — sorry, that’s not really the point.

The point is, yes, it affected work and home badly enough that I ended up being sent to the emergency department because I didn’t feel safe with my thoughts. So… yeah. It was more than just feeling sad.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1, F32.2, F32.3, F32.4\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I... I came in because I just can’t shake this heavy, hopeless feeling. It’s like everything I tried to build back up after the divorce just collapsed, and I’m sitting underneath all of it. I know that sounds dramatic, but that’s what it feels like. Like debris everywhere, and I’m supposed to just keep going to work and answer emails about drainage plans and concrete specs like any of it matters.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this heavy, hopeless feeling?\n\nPatient: About a month, I think. It sort of settled in around when the financial stuff from the divorce got really messy — the bills and accounts and all those stupid forms. I remember staring at a spreadsheet one night, and not even for work, just my own life, and thinking, “How is this what everything turned into?” Since then it’s just been... there. Like a weight.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like before this heavy feeling started?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I guess I haven’t really been paying attention to that. I used to like little things, like walking around near the river after work or looking at old building designs online — which sounds boring, I know, but I actually liked seeing how people solved things.\n\nLately it’s more like… I don’t even get that far. I’ll open a tab or put on a show and then realize I’m just staring at it. But I don’t know if that means I’m not enjoying it, or if I’m just distracted. Everything sort of blurs together.\n\nDoctor: Are there things that you still find pleasure in doing?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. That sounds like it should be an easy question, but I keep trying to think of something and then I get stuck on whether I actually enjoy it or I just do it because it’s there.\n\nLike, I made coffee this morning and I used to sort of like the routine of that — the grinder, the smell, all of it — but then I was just thinking about how the kitchen still has this one cabinet door that never closes right, and my ex always said we’d fix it, and then suddenly I’m not even drinking the coffee, I’m just standing there. So… I don’t know. Maybe little moments? But nothing that really feels like pleasure in the way it used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean… maybe? It’s hard to tell whether I’ve lost interest or whether everything just feels like too much effort to start.\n\nLike with the river walks, I used to do them without thinking, and now I’ll think, “I should go,” and then I remember I need to respond to an email or I see laundry on the chair, and suddenly it’s an hour later and I haven’t moved. But that could just be me being disorganized. I’ve always had piles of stuff when a project gets intense — at work we used to joke my desk looked like a landslide model, which, actually, was kind of accurate.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I haven’t really been reaching for those things, I guess. Whether that’s lost interest or just… getting stuck, I’m not totally sure.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still look forward to doing?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I keep thinking there should be something, like… even something small, but when I try to name it, my mind kind of wanders off.\n\nSometimes I think maybe I’ll look forward to finishing a work thing, but then I start thinking about this bridge project from years ago where we kept finding problems in the drainage plans, and everyone acted like it was a big mystery when it was obviously the grading, and— sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I don’t notice myself looking forward to much. Or if I do, it doesn’t stay in my head long enough for me to recognize it as that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about harming yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not exactly like… I don’t know. I haven’t been thinking, “I’m going to do this specific thing,” if that’s what you mean.\n\nBut I have been thinking it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up. Which sounds so dramatic when I say it out loud. I kept trying to tell myself it was just a passing thought, like background noise, but then it got… louder, I guess. Intense enough that I scared myself.\n\nI ended up calling the employee assistance program, actually. I almost didn’t, because their website is this awful maze of links and forms, and I kept thinking about how someone approved that layout somehow— sorry. The point is, yes. I have had thoughts like that, and it got bad enough that I reached out.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t been tracking it or anything. My scale is in the bathroom closet behind this stack of cleaning supplies I keep meaning to reorganize, so I don’t really use it.\n\nMy clothes… maybe they feel the same? I don’t know. I haven’t noticed a clear change, at least not one I could put a number on.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yeah. Sleep has been… pretty bad, honestly.\n\nSome nights I’m just lying there staring at the ceiling, going over bills or something I might’ve messed up at work, and then it turns into this whole mental spreadsheet I can’t shut off. Or I’ll finally sleep, but then I wake up before dawn and feel like I didn’t actually sleep at all.\n\nAnd then I start noticing stupid things, like the little crack in the ceiling paint, and I’m thinking about whether that’s moisture or just settling, because in buildings it’s never “just a crack,” and— sorry. Yes. I’ve been having trouble sleeping.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly than usual or feeling like you're speeding up in your thoughts or actions?\n\nPatient: More like speeding up, I think. Not moving slowly.\n\nI’ve been… pushing myself into everything. Like extra proposals at work, even ones that weren’t really mine to touch, and calling colleagues because I’d suddenly have some idea that felt important enough that I had to say it right then. Which is embarrassing, because people are busy, and also some of them don’t answer their phones anymore, they just let it go to voicemail like it’s 2009 or something.\n\nAnd I volunteered for tasks no one asked me to take on. Just kept adding things. Then at home, when there wasn’t anything useful left to do, I’d just pace around the apartment, kind of looking for the next thing. Like my body was still trying to solve a problem even after the problem was gone.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for things that have happened?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. A lot.\n\nI keep turning things over in my head and somehow it all becomes evidence that I’m just… bad at everything. Like I’ll think, I’m a terrible engineer, because of one calculation I missed or almost missed, or an email that sounded sharper than I meant it to. And then I start replaying every tense message, every decision, like I’m building a case against myself.\n\nAnd it’s not just work. I think I’m a failed wife, too. Like I should have been easier to live with, or more stable, or— I don’t know. Even little things become this proof that I ruin things. I feel like a burden. Then I’ll remember some random detail, like the wording in a project comment from months ago, and it just loops.\n\nDoctor: How often do you find yourself thinking you're a failure or that you're worthless?\n\nPatient: Pretty often. I mean… daily, probably. It’s like my brain keeps a running list of reasons I’m not doing anything right.\n\nI’ll tell myself I’m a terrible engineer because of one missed calculation or some email that came out too tense, and then I’ll start going back through everything like I’m checking a structure for cracks, except the structure is just me, I guess. And every little thing becomes proof.\n\nAnd with my marriage, I keep thinking I was a failed wife, that I was too much or not enough or just… a burden. It’s not like one thought and then it passes. It keeps coming back. Like I ruin things.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your heavy, hopeless feeling is getting worse over time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it has. Or maybe I only notice it now because it’s gotten so heavy.\n\nThat’s kind of why I came in. I can’t shake this hopeless feeling, like everything I tried to rebuild after the divorce just… collapsed. Like when a support fails and the whole thing comes down, except it’s my life, and I’m just sitting under all of it trying to pretend I can still function. And I know that sounds dramatic, but that’s how it feels.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling restless or fidgety lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… restless, I guess. Not just like tapping my foot, though I do that too sometimes. It’s more like I can’t leave things alone.\n\nI started throwing myself into extra proposals at work, calling people with ideas they didn’t even ask for, volunteering for tasks that weren’t mine. And then when there wasn’t anything useful left to do, I’d just pace around my apartment like I was waiting for some problem to appear so I could fix it.\n\nWhich is ridiculous because my apartment isn’t even that big, so it’s like… kitchen, window, hallway, back again. Like I’m inspecting a site with no actual site. But yes, restless. Definitely.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you're constantly on the go or can't sit still?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually a good way to put it — like I’m constantly “on,” even when there isn’t a real reason to be.\n\nAt work I started pushing myself into extra proposals, calling colleagues with new ideas, volunteering for tasks nobody asked me to take on. Which, I mean, sometimes that looks productive from the outside, so people don’t immediately question it. Engineers are always supposed to be busy, right? There’s always some deadline or drawing set or meeting that could’ve been an email.\n\nBut even when I got home, if there wasn’t anything useful left to do, I’d pace around my apartment. Just back and forth, like my body didn’t get the memo that the workday was over. It’s embarrassing because I’d catch myself doing it and think, okay, sit down like a normal person, and then two minutes later I’d be up again.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any thoughts or heard any voices that aren't your own?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not voices, like someone talking to me. I mean, I get thoughts that feel loud, if that makes sense, but they still feel like mine.\n\nSometimes when I’m stressed I’ll replay conversations in my head, especially work stuff — like a meeting where I should’ve said something differently, or the way someone paused before answering me, and then I start analyzing it like it’s a structural failure report. But that’s not the same as hearing something, I don’t think.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t heard voices that aren’t mine. At least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your heavy, hopeless feeling is interfering with your ability to function in daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. I mean, I wanted to say I was still functioning because I was still showing up, still answering emails, still putting on work clothes and pretending that counted. But it really wasn’t okay.\n\nAt work I started making calls I probably shouldn’t have been making — project decisions, judgment things — and then I’d fall apart in meetings. Not in some dramatic movie way, just… I couldn’t hold it together or think clearly. My supervisor actually had to take me off active project decisions, which was humiliating. Civil engineering is all about being reliable, you know, like if you miss one load calculation— sorry, that’s not the point.\n\nAt home it got worse too. I stopped keeping up with basic stuff, like routines, regular life things. Everything felt too heavy to do. And eventually I ended up being sent to the emergency department because I didn’t feel safe with my thoughts. So… yes. It’s interfering a lot.\n\nDoctor: Have you been fidgeting or having trouble sitting still lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. It’s not just fidgeting like tapping my foot, though I do that too sometimes — it’s more like I can’t leave things alone.\n\nI started taking on extra proposals at work, calling people with ideas, volunteering for tasks nobody asked me to do. And then I’d get home and there was no project, no meeting, no plan set to mark up, and I’d just pace around my apartment because sitting still felt… almost impossible.\n\nSorry, I know that’s kind of more work-related than what you asked, but yes — I’ve been having trouble sitting still.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant impairment in social, occupational, or other important areas of functioning due to your depressive symptoms?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… yes, pretty significantly.\n\nWork was where it showed up first, I think. I was still going in, but I wasn’t really functioning the way I’m supposed to. I started making questionable calls on project decisions, and in meetings I’d just kind of fall apart — not being able to think straight or keep myself together. My supervisor ended up taking me off active project decisions, which… in my field, that’s a big deal. It felt awful.\n\nAnd at home, it wasn’t like I was recovering there either. I stopped keeping up with basic routines, like normal daily stuff. Everything just slipped. I remember thinking, if my apartment were a project site, it would’ve been shut down immediately — sorry, that’s not really the point.\n\nThe point is, yes, it affected work and home badly enough that I ended up being sent to the emergency department because I didn’t feel safe with my thoughts. So… yeah. It was more than just feeling sad.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
